He didn't even hear it happen, and he has this huge German Shepherd that barks at literally everything, even the mailman. The mailman actually refuses to deliver packages to his porch anymore, he just leaves them at the end of the driveway in the rain...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I avoid those places. And if somebody starts talking about that night, I just shut it down or find a reason to leave the room. I just can't do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. I used to love preaching, playing the keys for worship, taking pictures, and especially running with the guys on the court. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I avoid it altogether because none of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just flat. \n\nLike with my camera, I used to take it everywhere. I have this really nice Canon DSLR, I saved up for like six months to buy it right after I finished my bachelor's degree. I even bought this special macro lens for it so I could take really close-up shots of flowers and bugs and stuff. I went to this botanical garden a couple years ago, over in the next county, and they had this whole exhibit on orchids. Orchids are so hard to keep alive, though. My mom got one for Mother's Day once, and it died within a week because she overwatered it. She always overwaters her plants. She has this big spider plant hanging in her living room that's just dripping water onto the carpet all the time. The carpet is this really ugly shag from the 70s, it's like a burnt orange color. She refuses to replace it because she says it's coming back into style, but I really don't think shag carpet is ever coming back. It's so hard to vacuum, too. You have to use the special attachment... \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't care about playing keys or any of that stuff anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Sleep? I mean, not really, no. I don't think I have trouble with it. I usually just go to bed and that's it. My mattress is pretty old, though. I've had it since I was in college, living in this tiny dorm room with a guy named Greg. Greg was a trip, man. He used to eat cereal out of a mixing bowl because he didn't want to do dishes. He'd just use the same bowl for days, it was so gross. We had this mini-fridge that constantly hummed, like a really low-pitched buzzing sound. It drove me crazy when I was trying to study for my theology finals. Theology is a lot of reading, you know? Just stacks and stacks of books. I still have most of them on this bookshelf in my office at the church, but the shelf is bowing in the middle because I bought cheap particle board instead of solid wood. You really get what you pay for with furniture. I was at Ikea a few months ago looking for a new desk, and the place is basically a maze. You go in for one thing and come out with Swedish meatballs and a bunch of tea lights you don't need... \n\nBut yeah, sleep is fine. I don't really notice any issues with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like a sudden noise?\n\nPatient: Startled? No, not really. I mean, I don't jump out of my skin or anything if a door slams. Speaking of doors, the front door at the parsonage is so drafty. It's this heavy oak thing, probably original to the house, which was built in like, 1920 or something. It has this beautiful stained glass window in the center, though. It's a picture of a dove with an olive branch. The colors are amazing when the sun hits it just right in the late afternoon. But the weather stripping is completely shot. I tried fixing it myself with this foam tape I bought at Home Depot, but it just peeled right off the next day. Home Depot is always so overwhelming anyway. The aisles are so long, and I can never find anyone in an orange apron to help me. I was in there looking for lightbulbs last week—just regular 60-watt bulbs for my reading lamp—and I ended up in the plumbing aisle looking at PVC pipes for twenty minutes. I don't even know anything about plumbing. My dad was always the handy one. He could fix anything with a roll of duct tape and a Swiss Army knife. He used to carry that knife everywhere, even to church... \n\nBut no, sudden noises don't really bother me. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess I do. It's like I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. If I go to a restaurant, I have to be in a booth facing the entrance. I went to this diner last week, the one down on 4th Street that serves breakfast all day. They make these incredible blueberry pancakes, like, the blueberries are actually cooked into the batter, not just dumped on top. I hate when places just dump cold fruit on top of a hot pancake. It ruins the whole temperature dynamic, you know? Anyway, they tried to seat me in the middle of the room and I just... I couldn't do it. I had to ask for a corner booth. \n\nAnd at church events, it's really bad. I'm supposed to be fellowshipping, but I'm always watching people's hands, or scanning the corners of the room. If a car slows down outside the window, my eyes just dart right to it. I'm constantly mapping out where the nearest exit is instead of actually paying attention to the person talking to me. It's exhausting. Like, last Sunday, Mrs. Higgins was telling me about her grandson's piano recital. He's been playing for three years, apparently. I think he's learning some Chopin right now, which is pretty ambitious for a ten-year-old. Chopin has all those crazy trills and complex time signatures. I tried learning the \"Minute Waltz\" when I was a teenager, but my hands just aren't fast enough. You have to have really nimble fingers for that kind of stuff. My piano teacher, Mr. Davies, used to make me do these Hanon exercises for like thirty minutes before we even started a piece. He was so strict. He always smelled like peppermint and old paper... \n\nBut yeah, I'm always just... scanning. Watching. I can't just relax and be present.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your cousin's death that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Man, I don't know. I used to love preaching, you know? Getting up there and sharing the Word, connecting with the congregation. And playing the keys during worship... I used to get so lost in the music. I actually have this really nice Roland keyboard at home. It's an RD-2000, fully weighted keys, so it feels exactly like a real grand piano. I saved up for like a year to buy it. I used to practice on it every night. The action on those keys is just incredible, and the built-in piano sounds are so rich. They sampled actual concert grands for it. I bought this heavy-duty stand for it, too, because it weighs a ton. It's one of those Z-stands, super stable. I got it online from Sweetwater. They always send a little bag of candy with your order, which is such a weird but cool marketing thing. I usually eat the Bit-O-Honey first. Bit-O-Honey is so underrated, honestly. People always go for the chocolate, but there's something about that chewy honey and almond flavor... \n\nBut yeah, the preaching, the keyboard... taking pictures, too. I used to do a lot of photography. And I'd play pickup basketball with the guys on the court behind the church. But now? Now I mostly just go through the motions. Or I avoid it altogether. None of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death itself, like you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. All the time. I keep... I keep telling myself that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, or if I had walked him out to his car, he'd still be here. Or if I had just kept a better watch over those boys... I was supposed to be in charge. I'm the youth pastor. I'm supposed to be the responsible one. Being a youth pastor is a lot harder than people think, you know? They think it's all just playing dodgeball and eating pizza. We used to order from this place called Tony's down on 5th Avenue. Best garlic knots in the city. They make them from scratch every morning, and they just drench them in this butter and garlic sauce. The owner, Tony—well, his name is actually Anthony, but everyone calls him Tony—he always gives us a discount because it's for the church. He's a really nice guy. He's got this huge mustache that looks like something out of an old western movie. My grandfather used to have a mustache like that. He was a mechanic. He worked on classic cars. He had this beautiful '67 Mustang in his garage that he spent years restoring. It was cherry red with white racing stripes. The engine sounded like a beast when you revved it. He taught me how to change the oil when I was like, eight years old. You have to make sure you put the drain plug back in tight, but not too tight, or you'll strip the threads. And getting the oil filter off can be a nightmare if it's stuck...\n\nBut yeah, I just... I should have done something differently. If I had just paid more attention, he wouldn't be gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don't know... I'm not really sure if I expect bad things all the time. I mean, it's hard to say what's normal anymore. Everything just feels kind of... off. Like when you're watching a movie and the audio is just a split second behind the video? That used to drive me crazy when I'd try to watch sermons online. We actually had to upgrade the whole A/V system at the church because the livestream kept lagging. We got this new digital mixer, a Behringer X32. It's got like 32 channels and motorized faders, which is super cool to watch when you switch scenes. The faders just zip up and down on their own. It took me like three weeks to figure out how to route the monitors properly. I had to watch all these YouTube tutorials from this guy who wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts. I don't know why, but sound engineers always seem to wear Hawaiian shirts or cargo shorts. It's like a uniform. My uncle used to wear cargo shorts all the time. He said he needed the pockets for all his tools, but he mostly just kept peppermints and golf tees in them. He loved golf. He'd go out every Sunday afternoon, even if it was raining. He said the rain made the greens softer, which helped his short game. I went with him once, but I'm terrible at golf. I always slice the ball into the woods...

But anyway, expecting bad things? I don't know. I just... I don't really know.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, um, thank you for seeing me. I guess what brings me in is... And it happens at the worst times, like even when I'm just standing in the church office trying to act normal. \n\nI work at the church, you know, I'm on the pastoral staff. The office is actually in this really old annex building, it used to be a schoolhouse back in the twenties, I think. The plumbing in there is just a complete nightmare. It makes these loud groaning noises all day, and we had a guy out last Tuesday who said we'd have to rip up the entire floor to fix the main pipe. Which, you know, it's original hardwood, so the senior pastor is going to have an absolute fit if we have to destroy it right before the big community fundraiser we do every year. We usually do a bake sale and a silent auction, and people really love the auction part... \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what was I saying? Right, the... the lights and the shouting. Yeah. I'll just be standing there by the copier or something, trying to be normal, and suddenly I'm right back there on the pavement with him.\n\nDoctor: What happened to your cousin on the pavement?\n\nPatient: It... I don't really know. It's hard to even explain it. I just... my cousin, he... he's actually the one who taught me how to play guitar. We used to play together all the time back home. He had this beat-up old acoustic he got at a garage sale, and the strings were so rusted you'd practically need a tetanus shot just to tune the thing, but he loved it. \n\nHe was always fixing things up like that. Actually, he was supposed to help me build this big wooden shelving unit for the youth room at our church. We have all these donated paperbacks and devotionals just sitting in cardboard boxes right now, and it looks really awful. The youth pastor, Mark, he’s been on my case about getting it organized, but Mark doesn't really get how expensive lumber is right now. He wants everything done immediately. He’s a good guy, but man, he drinks like six cups of espresso before noon and just vibrates. I tried buying that half-caff blend for the breakroom to see if he'd notice, but the church secretary got really mad because she likes the strong stuff, and she's been there for like thirty years so you do not want to cross her. She actually knitted me a sweater when I graduated with my bachelor's, which was sweet, but it's neon orange... \n\nSo, yeah. I just... I don't know how to describe what happened. It was just a lot of shouting.\n\nDoctor: Did your cousin die on the pavement?\n\nPatient: Yeah, he... he did. He died right there. It was just... I don't know, it's so hard to even think about. We were supposed to go to this music festival later that summer, you know? We had already bought the tickets and everything. They were non-refundable, which I found out when I tried to call the ticket company after... well, after. \n\nThe customer service lady was actually really nice about it, though. She said she couldn't give me the money back because of their policy, but she sent me this voucher for a future event. I haven't used it. I don't even know who I'd go with now. Most of my friends from college moved out of state, and the people I work with at the church aren't really the music festival type. Except maybe Mark, the youth pastor, but I feel like if I took Mark to a festival he'd just try to start a drum circle or something and embarrass me. He bought this djembe online last month and he keeps bringing it to staff meetings. It's so loud. \n\nBut yeah... he died. Right on the pavement.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling since your cousin died?\n\nPatient: It's just... most days I feel this heavy mix of fear and anger, and just so much guilt sitting right here in my chest. From the second I wake up, it's just there. It feels like I failed God, and my family, and... and even the kids at church who trust me. \n\nSpeaking of the kids, we actually had to cancel the middle school lock-in last weekend because we couldn't get enough chaperones. Parents are just so busy right now, which I get, but it's really frustrating when you've ordered fifteen large pizzas. We got them from that place down on 4th Street, Mario's. They make this incredible garlic knot crust, but it's super greasy. Anyway, I ended up having to take like eight boxes of pizza home and put them in my freezer. My freezer is tiny, it's one of those little apartment-sized refrigerators because my kitchen doesn't have the space for a full-size one. The landlord said he was going to remodel the kitchen before I moved in, but all he did was paint the cabinets this awful mustard yellow color. He said it was \"retro,\" but it really just looks like baby food. I've been trying to find some contact paper to cover it up, maybe something with a nice faux-marble finish, but the hardware store only had floral patterns...\n\nBut yeah, the guilt and the fear. It's just always there, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks to the scene with your cousin?\n\nPatient: Well, they started... it's been going on since he was killed, which was two years and three months ago. Exactly two years and three months. I remember because it happened right before the big autumn bake sale at the church, and I was supposed to be in charge of the cash box. I ended up having to ask Mrs. Higgins to cover for me, and she's lovely, but she is terrible at math. She gave someone back a twenty-dollar bill instead of a one, and we ended up losing money on the whole event. We were trying to raise funds for a new projector for the sanctuary because the old one keeps flickering right in the middle of the sermon. It makes Pastor Dave look like he's in a strobe light, which is really distracting when he's trying to talk about something serious like forgiveness. \n\nDave actually suggested we try doing a car wash instead to make up the funds, but the water pressure at the church is awful. The pipes are from like the 1950s. The plumber said they all need to be replaced, but that would cost thousands of dollars, and we just don't have it in the budget right now, especially since we had to fix the roof over the fellowship hall last spring... \n\nBut yeah, two years and three months. That's how long it's been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your cousin?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I take the long way around town now so I don't have to pass the community center or that basketball court. It adds like twenty minutes to my drive, which is really annoying because gas prices are just ridiculous right now. I was at the pump the other day and it was almost four dollars a gallon. I drive this old Honda Civic, it's from 2008, so it usually gets pretty good mileage, but the check engine light has been on for like three weeks. I took it to this mechanic, Tony, who goes to our church. He's a nice guy, sings in the choir—he's a bass, actually, has this really deep, booming voice. But he said I need a new catalytic converter, and those things are so expensive. People keep stealing them, too. My neighbor had his stolen right out of his driveway in the middle of the night. He didn't even hear it happen, and he has this huge German Shepherd that barks at literally everything, even the mailman. The mailman actually refuses to deliver packages to his porch anymore, he just leaves them at the end of the driveway in the rain...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I avoid those places. And if somebody starts talking about that night, I just shut it down or find a reason to leave the room. I just can't do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. I used to love preaching, playing the keys for worship, taking pictures, and especially running with the guys on the court. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I avoid it altogether because none of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just flat. \n\nLike with my camera, I used to take it everywhere. I have this really nice Canon DSLR, I saved up for like six months to buy it right after I finished my bachelor's degree. I even bought this special macro lens for it so I could take really close-up shots of flowers and bugs and stuff. I went to this botanical garden a couple years ago, over in the next county, and they had this whole exhibit on orchids. Orchids are so hard to keep alive, though. My mom got one for Mother's Day once, and it died within a week because she overwatered it. She always overwaters her plants. She has this big spider plant hanging in her living room that's just dripping water onto the carpet all the time. The carpet is this really ugly shag from the 70s, it's like a burnt orange color. She refuses to replace it because she says it's coming back into style, but I really don't think shag carpet is ever coming back. It's so hard to vacuum, too. You have to use the special attachment... \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't care about playing keys or any of that stuff anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Sleep? I mean, not really, no. I don't think I have trouble with it. I usually just go to bed and that's it. My mattress is pretty old, though. I've had it since I was in college, living in this tiny dorm room with a guy named Greg. Greg was a trip, man. He used to eat cereal out of a mixing bowl because he didn't want to do dishes. He'd just use the same bowl for days, it was so gross. We had this mini-fridge that constantly hummed, like a really low-pitched buzzing sound. It drove me crazy when I was trying to study for my theology finals. Theology is a lot of reading, you know? Just stacks and stacks of books. I still have most of them on this bookshelf in my office at the church, but the shelf is bowing in the middle because I bought cheap particle board instead of solid wood. You really get what you pay for with furniture. I was at Ikea a few months ago looking for a new desk, and the place is basically a maze. You go in for one thing and come out with Swedish meatballs and a bunch of tea lights you don't need... \n\nBut yeah, sleep is fine. I don't really notice any issues with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like a sudden noise?\n\nPatient: Startled? No, not really. I mean, I don't jump out of my skin or anything if a door slams. Speaking of doors, the front door at the parsonage is so drafty. It's this heavy oak thing, probably original to the house, which was built in like, 1920 or something. It has this beautiful stained glass window in the center, though. It's a picture of a dove with an olive branch. The colors are amazing when the sun hits it just right in the late afternoon. But the weather stripping is completely shot. I tried fixing it myself with this foam tape I bought at Home Depot, but it just peeled right off the next day. Home Depot is always so overwhelming anyway. The aisles are so long, and I can never find anyone in an orange apron to help me. I was in there looking for lightbulbs last week—just regular 60-watt bulbs for my reading lamp—and I ended up in the plumbing aisle looking at PVC pipes for twenty minutes. I don't even know anything about plumbing. My dad was always the handy one. He could fix anything with a roll of duct tape and a Swiss Army knife. He used to carry that knife everywhere, even to church... \n\nBut no, sudden noises don't really bother me. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess I do. It's like I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. If I go to a restaurant, I have to be in a booth facing the entrance. I went to this diner last week, the one down on 4th Street that serves breakfast all day. They make these incredible blueberry pancakes, like, the blueberries are actually cooked into the batter, not just dumped on top. I hate when places just dump cold fruit on top of a hot pancake. It ruins the whole temperature dynamic, you know? Anyway, they tried to seat me in the middle of the room and I just... I couldn't do it. I had to ask for a corner booth. \n\nAnd at church events, it's really bad. I'm supposed to be fellowshipping, but I'm always watching people's hands, or scanning the corners of the room. If a car slows down outside the window, my eyes just dart right to it. I'm constantly mapping out where the nearest exit is instead of actually paying attention to the person talking to me. It's exhausting. Like, last Sunday, Mrs. Higgins was telling me about her grandson's piano recital. He's been playing for three years, apparently. I think he's learning some Chopin right now, which is pretty ambitious for a ten-year-old. Chopin has all those crazy trills and complex time signatures. I tried learning the \"Minute Waltz\" when I was a teenager, but my hands just aren't fast enough. You have to have really nimble fingers for that kind of stuff. My piano teacher, Mr. Davies, used to make me do these Hanon exercises for like thirty minutes before we even started a piece. He was so strict. He always smelled like peppermint and old paper... \n\nBut yeah, I'm always just... scanning. Watching. I can't just relax and be present.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your cousin's death that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Man, I don't know. I used to love preaching, you know? Getting up there and sharing the Word, connecting with the congregation. And playing the keys during worship... I used to get so lost in the music. I actually have this really nice Roland keyboard at home. It's an RD-2000, fully weighted keys, so it feels exactly like a real grand piano. I saved up for like a year to buy it. I used to practice on it every night. The action on those keys is just incredible, and the built-in piano sounds are so rich. They sampled actual concert grands for it. I bought this heavy-duty stand for it, too, because it weighs a ton. It's one of those Z-stands, super stable. I got it online from Sweetwater. They always send a little bag of candy with your order, which is such a weird but cool marketing thing. I usually eat the Bit-O-Honey first. Bit-O-Honey is so underrated, honestly. People always go for the chocolate, but there's something about that chewy honey and almond flavor... \n\nBut yeah, the preaching, the keyboard... taking pictures, too. I used to do a lot of photography. And I'd play pickup basketball with the guys on the court behind the church. But now? Now I mostly just go through the motions. Or I avoid it altogether. None of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death itself, like you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. All the time. I keep... I keep telling myself that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, or if I had walked him out to his car, he'd still be here. Or if I had just kept a better watch over those boys... I was supposed to be in charge. I'm the youth pastor. I'm supposed to be the responsible one. Being a youth pastor is a lot harder than people think, you know? They think it's all just playing dodgeball and eating pizza. We used to order from this place called Tony's down on 5th Avenue. Best garlic knots in the city. They make them from scratch every morning, and they just drench them in this butter and garlic sauce. The owner, Tony—well, his name is actually Anthony, but everyone calls him Tony—he always gives us a discount because it's for the church. He's a really nice guy. He's got this huge mustache that looks like something out of an old western movie. My grandfather used to have a mustache like that. He was a mechanic. He worked on classic cars. He had this beautiful '67 Mustang in his garage that he spent years restoring. It was cherry red with white racing stripes. The engine sounded like a beast when you revved it. He taught me how to change the oil when I was like, eight years old. You have to make sure you put the drain plug back in tight, but not too tight, or you'll strip the threads. And getting the oil filter off can be a nightmare if it's stuck...\n\nBut yeah, I just... I should have done something differently. If I had just paid more attention, he wouldn't be gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always expecting something bad to happen, even when things seem to be going well?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I'm not really sure if I expect bad things all the time. I mean, it's hard to say what's normal anymore. Everything just feels kind of... off. Like when you're watching a movie and the audio is just a split second behind the video? That used to drive me crazy when I'd try to watch sermons online. We actually had to upgrade the whole A/V system at the church because the livestream kept lagging. We got this new digital mixer, a Behringer X32. It's got like 32 channels and motorized faders, which is super cool to watch when you switch scenes. The faders just zip up and down on their own. It took me like three weeks to figure out how to route the monitors properly. I had to watch all these YouTube tutorials from this guy who wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts. I don't know why, but sound engineers always seem to wear Hawaiian shirts or cargo shorts. It's like a uniform. My uncle used to wear cargo shorts all the time. He said he needed the pockets for all his tools, but he mostly just kept peppermints and golf tees in them. He loved golf. He'd go out every Sunday afternoon, even if it was raining. He said the rain made the greens softer, which helped his short game. I went with him once, but I'm terrible at golf. I always slice the ball into the woods...\n\nBut anyway, expecting bad things? I don't know. I just... I don't really know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah, I do. I mean, I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. It's just... I have to see who's coming in. And when I'm at church events now, I'm not even paying attention to the people in front of me. I'm just always watching hands, looking at corners, noticing if a car slows down outside. He was this really gruff guy named Steve. He made us change out all the batteries in the emergency exit signs because they were like, a week past their expiration date. We had to buy these special 6-volt batteries that you can't just get at a regular grocery store. I had to drive all the way out to the industrial park to this specialty battery warehouse. It's this massive building, just aisles and aisles of batteries. They even had those huge ones for forklifts. Forklifts are so dangerous, honestly. My buddy Dave used to drive a forklift at a warehouse, and he said guys would always try to do tricks on them, like popping wheelies. Which is insane because those things weigh thousands of pounds. If one tips over on you, you're done. He said they had to watch these super cheesy safety videos from the 90s where the actors would dramatically overreact to getting their toes run over... 

But yeah, I'm just always watching. Always looking for the exits, watching people's hands. It's exhausting.

They make this incredible garlic knot crust, but it's super greasy. Anyway, I ended up having to take like eight boxes of pizza home and put them in my freezer. My freezer is tiny, it's one of those little apartment-sized refrigerators because my kitchen doesn't have the space for a full-size one. The landlord said he was going to remodel the kitchen before I moved in, but all he did was paint the cabinets this awful mustard yellow color. He said it was \"retro,\" but it really just looks like baby food. I've been trying to find some contact paper to cover it up, maybe something with a nice faux-marble finish, but the hardware store only had floral patterns...\n\nBut yeah, the guilt and the fear. It's just always there, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks to the scene with your cousin?\n\nPatient: Well, they started... it's been going on since he was killed, which was two years and three months ago. Exactly two years and three months. I remember because it happened right before the big autumn bake sale at the church, and I was supposed to be in charge of the cash box. I ended up having to ask Mrs. Higgins to cover for me, and she's lovely, but she is terrible at math. She gave someone back a twenty-dollar bill instead of a one, and we ended up losing money on the whole event. We were trying to raise funds for a new projector for the sanctuary because the old one keeps flickering right in the middle of the sermon. It makes Pastor Dave look like he's in a strobe light, which is really distracting when he's trying to talk about something serious like forgiveness. \n\nDave actually suggested we try doing a car wash instead to make up the funds, but the water pressure at the church is awful. The pipes are from like the 1950s. The plumber said they all need to be replaced, but that would cost thousands of dollars, and we just don't have it in the budget right now, especially since we had to fix the roof over the fellowship hall last spring... \n\nBut yeah, two years and three months. That's how long it's been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your cousin?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I take the long way around town now so I don't have to pass the community center or that basketball court. It adds like twenty minutes to my drive, which is really annoying because gas prices are just ridiculous right now. I was at the pump the other day and it was almost four dollars a gallon. I drive this old Honda Civic, it's from 2008, so it usually gets pretty good mileage, but the check engine light has been on for like three weeks. I took it to this mechanic, Tony, who goes to our church. He's a nice guy, sings in the choir—he's a bass, actually, has this really deep, booming voice. But he said I need a new catalytic converter, and those things are so expensive. People keep stealing them, too. My neighbor had his stolen right out of his driveway in the middle of the night. He didn't even hear it happen, and he has this huge German Shepherd that barks at literally everything, even the mailman. The mailman actually refuses to deliver packages to his porch anymore, he just leaves them at the end of the driveway in the rain...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I avoid those places. And if somebody starts talking about that night, I just shut it down or find a reason to leave the room. I just can't do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. I used to love preaching, playing the keys for worship, taking pictures, and especially running with the guys on the court. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I avoid it altogether because none of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just flat. \n\nLike with my camera, I used to take it everywhere. I have this really nice Canon DSLR, I saved up for like six months to buy it right after I finished my bachelor's degree. I even bought this special macro lens for it so I could take really close-up shots of flowers and bugs and stuff. I went to this botanical garden a couple years ago, over in the next county, and they had this whole exhibit on orchids. Orchids are so hard to keep alive, though. My mom got one for Mother's Day once, and it died within a week because she overwatered it. She always overwaters her plants. She has this big spider plant hanging in her living room that's just dripping water onto the carpet all the time. The carpet is this really ugly shag from the 70s, it's like a burnt orange color. She refuses to replace it because she says it's coming back into style, but I really don't think shag carpet is ever coming back. It's so hard to vacuum, too. You have to use the special attachment... \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't care about playing keys or any of that stuff anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Sleep? I mean, not really, no. I don't think I have trouble with it. I usually just go to bed and that's it. My mattress is pretty old, though. I've had it since I was in college, living in this tiny dorm room with a guy named Greg. Greg was a trip, man. He used to eat cereal out of a mixing bowl because he didn't want to do dishes. He'd just use the same bowl for days, it was so gross. We had this mini-fridge that constantly hummed, like a really low-pitched buzzing sound. It drove me crazy when I was trying to study for my theology finals. Theology is a lot of reading, you know? Just stacks and stacks of books. I still have most of them on this bookshelf in my office at the church, but the shelf is bowing in the middle because I bought cheap particle board instead of solid wood. You really get what you pay for with furniture. I was at Ikea a few months ago looking for a new desk, and the place is basically a maze. You go in for one thing and come out with Swedish meatballs and a bunch of tea lights you don't need... \n\nBut yeah, sleep is fine. I don't really notice any issues with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like a sudden noise?\n\nPatient: Startled? No, not really. I mean, I don't jump out of my skin or anything if a door slams. Speaking of doors, the front door at the parsonage is so drafty. It's this heavy oak thing, probably original to the house, which was built in like, 1920 or something. It has this beautiful stained glass window in the center, though. It's a picture of a dove with an olive branch. The colors are amazing when the sun hits it just right in the late afternoon. But the weather stripping is completely shot. I tried fixing it myself with this foam tape I bought at Home Depot, but it just peeled right off the next day. Home Depot is always so overwhelming anyway. The aisles are so long, and I can never find anyone in an orange apron to help me. I was in there looking for lightbulbs last week—just regular 60-watt bulbs for my reading lamp—and I ended up in the plumbing aisle looking at PVC pipes for twenty minutes. I don't even know anything about plumbing. My dad was always the handy one. He could fix anything with a roll of duct tape and a Swiss Army knife. He used to carry that knife everywhere, even to church... \n\nBut no, sudden noises don't really bother me. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess I do. It's like I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. If I go to a restaurant, I have to be in a booth facing the entrance. I went to this diner last week, the one down on 4th Street that serves breakfast all day. They make these incredible blueberry pancakes, like, the blueberries are actually cooked into the batter, not just dumped on top. I hate when places just dump cold fruit on top of a hot pancake. It ruins the whole temperature dynamic, you know? Anyway, they tried to seat me in the middle of the room and I just... I couldn't do it. I had to ask for a corner booth. \n\nAnd at church events, it's really bad. I'm supposed to be fellowshipping, but I'm always watching people's hands, or scanning the corners of the room. If a car slows down outside the window, my eyes just dart right to it. I'm constantly mapping out where the nearest exit is instead of actually paying attention to the person talking to me. It's exhausting. Like, last Sunday, Mrs. Higgins was telling me about her grandson's piano recital. He's been playing for three years, apparently. I think he's learning some Chopin right now, which is pretty ambitious for a ten-year-old. Chopin has all those crazy trills and complex time signatures. I tried learning the \"Minute Waltz\" when I was a teenager, but my hands just aren't fast enough. You have to have really nimble fingers for that kind of stuff. My piano teacher, Mr. Davies, used to make me do these Hanon exercises for like thirty minutes before we even started a piece. He was so strict. He always smelled like peppermint and old paper... \n\nBut yeah, I'm always just... scanning. Watching. I can't just relax and be present.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your cousin's death that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Man, I don't know. I used to love preaching, you know? Getting up there and sharing the Word, connecting with the congregation. And playing the keys during worship... I used to get so lost in the music. I actually have this really nice Roland keyboard at home. It's an RD-2000, fully weighted keys, so it feels exactly like a real grand piano. I saved up for like a year to buy it. I used to practice on it every night. The action on those keys is just incredible, and the built-in piano sounds are so rich. They sampled actual concert grands for it. I bought this heavy-duty stand for it, too, because it weighs a ton. It's one of those Z-stands, super stable. I got it online from Sweetwater. They always send a little bag of candy with your order, which is such a weird but cool marketing thing. I usually eat the Bit-O-Honey first. Bit-O-Honey is so underrated, honestly. People always go for the chocolate, but there's something about that chewy honey and almond flavor... \n\nBut yeah, the preaching, the keyboard... taking pictures, too. I used to do a lot of photography. And I'd play pickup basketball with the guys on the court behind the church. But now? Now I mostly just go through the motions. Or I avoid it altogether. None of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death itself, like you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. All the time. I keep... I keep telling myself that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, or if I had walked him out to his car, he'd still be here. Or if I had just kept a better watch over those boys... I was supposed to be in charge. I'm the youth pastor. I'm supposed to be the responsible one. Being a youth pastor is a lot harder than people think, you know? They think it's all just playing dodgeball and eating pizza. We used to order from this place called Tony's down on 5th Avenue. Best garlic knots in the city. They make them from scratch every morning, and they just drench them in this butter and garlic sauce. The owner, Tony—well, his name is actually Anthony, but everyone calls him Tony—he always gives us a discount because it's for the church. He's a really nice guy. He's got this huge mustache that looks like something out of an old western movie. My grandfather used to have a mustache like that. He was a mechanic. He worked on classic cars. He had this beautiful '67 Mustang in his garage that he spent years restoring. It was cherry red with white racing stripes. The engine sounded like a beast when you revved it. He taught me how to change the oil when I was like, eight years old. You have to make sure you put the drain plug back in tight, but not too tight, or you'll strip the threads. And getting the oil filter off can be a nightmare if it's stuck...\n\nBut yeah, I just... I should have done something differently. If I had just paid more attention, he wouldn't be gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always expecting something bad to happen, even when things seem to be going well?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I'm not really sure if I expect bad things all the time. I mean, it's hard to say what's normal anymore. Everything just feels kind of... off. Like when you're watching a movie and the audio is just a split second behind the video? That used to drive me crazy when I'd try to watch sermons online. We actually had to upgrade the whole A/V system at the church because the livestream kept lagging. We got this new digital mixer, a Behringer X32. It's got like 32 channels and motorized faders, which is super cool to watch when you switch scenes. The faders just zip up and down on their own. It took me like three weeks to figure out how to route the monitors properly. I had to watch all these YouTube tutorials from this guy who wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts. I don't know why, but sound engineers always seem to wear Hawaiian shirts or cargo shorts. It's like a uniform. My uncle used to wear cargo shorts all the time. He said he needed the pockets for all his tools, but he mostly just kept peppermints and golf tees in them. He loved golf. He'd go out every Sunday afternoon, even if it was raining. He said the rain made the greens softer, which helped his short game. I went with him once, but I'm terrible at golf. I always slice the ball into the woods...\n\nBut anyway, expecting bad things? I don't know. I just... I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent threat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. It's just... I have to see who's coming in. And when I'm at church events now, I'm not even paying attention to the people in front of me. I'm just always watching hands, looking at corners, noticing if a car slows down outside. I'm constantly scoping out where the nearest exit is. We actually have four exits in the main sanctuary, which is good for fire code. The fire marshal came by last month to do an inspection. He was this really gruff guy named Steve. He made us change out all the batteries in the emergency exit signs because they were like, a week past their expiration date. We had to buy these special 6-volt batteries that you can't just get at a regular grocery store. I had to drive all the way out to the industrial park to this specialty battery warehouse. It's this massive building, just aisles and aisles of batteries. They even had those huge ones for forklifts. Forklifts are so dangerous, honestly. My buddy Dave used to drive a forklift at a warehouse, and he said guys would always try to do tricks on them, like popping wheelies. Which is insane because those things weigh thousands of pounds. If one tips over on you, you're done. He said they had to watch these super cheesy safety videos from the 90s where the actors would dramatically overreact to getting their toes run over... \n\nBut yeah, I'm just always watching. Always looking for the exits, watching people's hands. It's exhausting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I... I really have been. It's bad. I've snapped in ministry meetings over things that shouldn't even matter. Just last week, I raised my voice at the deacons and a couple of the youth leaders over the scheduling for the fall retreat. I was talking to them like they were the enemy, you know? Just completely went off on them. And afterward, I just hate how fast I went there. I never used to be like that. The fall retreat is usually my favorite thing to plan. We go up to this camp in the mountains, Camp Cedar Ridge. It's beautiful up there in October when the leaves are changing. The camp director, his name is Pastor Mike, he's been running the place for like thirty years. He has this massive golden retriever named Samson who just wanders around the camp all day begging for hot dogs. The kids love him. We always do a big bonfire on the last night, and we make s'mores. But you have to be careful with the marshmallows because if you get them too close to the coals, they just turn into a fireball. My little sister used to do that on purpose when we were kids. She'd catch her marshmallow on fire and then wave it around like a torch until my dad yelled at her. He was always so strict about fire safety. He was a volunteer firefighter for a while in our hometown. They had this old siren on top of the firehouse that would go off whenever there was a call, and it was so loud you could hear it from miles away. It sounded like an air raid siren from World War II. I used to watch all these documentaries on the History Channel about World War II with my grandpa...

But yeah, the anger... I just snap at people now. I don't mean to, but it just happens so fast.

I've been trying to find some contact paper to cover it up, maybe something with a nice faux-marble finish, but the hardware store only had floral patterns...\n\nBut yeah, the guilt and the fear. It's just always there, every single day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these flashbacks to the scene with your cousin?\n\nPatient: Well, they started... it's been going on since he was killed, which was two years and three months ago. Exactly two years and three months. I remember because it happened right before the big autumn bake sale at the church, and I was supposed to be in charge of the cash box. I ended up having to ask Mrs. Higgins to cover for me, and she's lovely, but she is terrible at math. She gave someone back a twenty-dollar bill instead of a one, and we ended up losing money on the whole event. We were trying to raise funds for a new projector for the sanctuary because the old one keeps flickering right in the middle of the sermon. It makes Pastor Dave look like he's in a strobe light, which is really distracting when he's trying to talk about something serious like forgiveness. \n\nDave actually suggested we try doing a car wash instead to make up the funds, but the water pressure at the church is awful. The pipes are from like the 1950s. The plumber said they all need to be replaced, but that would cost thousands of dollars, and we just don't have it in the budget right now, especially since we had to fix the roof over the fellowship hall last spring... \n\nBut yeah, two years and three months. That's how long it's been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of your cousin?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I take the long way around town now so I don't have to pass the community center or that basketball court. It adds like twenty minutes to my drive, which is really annoying because gas prices are just ridiculous right now. I was at the pump the other day and it was almost four dollars a gallon. I drive this old Honda Civic, it's from 2008, so it usually gets pretty good mileage, but the check engine light has been on for like three weeks. I took it to this mechanic, Tony, who goes to our church. He's a nice guy, sings in the choir—he's a bass, actually, has this really deep, booming voice. But he said I need a new catalytic converter, and those things are so expensive. People keep stealing them, too. My neighbor had his stolen right out of his driveway in the middle of the night. He didn't even hear it happen, and he has this huge German Shepherd that barks at literally everything, even the mailman. The mailman actually refuses to deliver packages to his porch anymore, he just leaves them at the end of the driveway in the rain...\n\nBut anyway, yeah, I avoid those places. And if somebody starts talking about that night, I just shut it down or find a reason to leave the room. I just can't do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like playing guitar?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. I used to love preaching, playing the keys for worship, taking pictures, and especially running with the guys on the court. But now I mostly just go through the motions, or I avoid it altogether because none of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just flat. \n\nLike with my camera, I used to take it everywhere. I have this really nice Canon DSLR, I saved up for like six months to buy it right after I finished my bachelor's degree. I even bought this special macro lens for it so I could take really close-up shots of flowers and bugs and stuff. I went to this botanical garden a couple years ago, over in the next county, and they had this whole exhibit on orchids. Orchids are so hard to keep alive, though. My mom got one for Mother's Day once, and it died within a week because she overwatered it. She always overwaters her plants. She has this big spider plant hanging in her living room that's just dripping water onto the carpet all the time. The carpet is this really ugly shag from the 70s, it's like a burnt orange color. She refuses to replace it because she says it's coming back into style, but I really don't think shag carpet is ever coming back. It's so hard to vacuum, too. You have to use the special attachment... \n\nBut yeah, I just... I don't care about playing keys or any of that stuff anymore. It's just gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling or staying asleep?\n\nPatient: Sleep? I mean, not really, no. I don't think I have trouble with it. I usually just go to bed and that's it. My mattress is pretty old, though. I've had it since I was in college, living in this tiny dorm room with a guy named Greg. Greg was a trip, man. He used to eat cereal out of a mixing bowl because he didn't want to do dishes. He'd just use the same bowl for days, it was so gross. We had this mini-fridge that constantly hummed, like a really low-pitched buzzing sound. It drove me crazy when I was trying to study for my theology finals. Theology is a lot of reading, you know? Just stacks and stacks of books. I still have most of them on this bookshelf in my office at the church, but the shelf is bowing in the middle because I bought cheap particle board instead of solid wood. You really get what you pay for with furniture. I was at Ikea a few months ago looking for a new desk, and the place is basically a maze. You go in for one thing and come out with Swedish meatballs and a bunch of tea lights you don't need... \n\nBut yeah, sleep is fine. I don't really notice any issues with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like a sudden noise?\n\nPatient: Startled? No, not really. I mean, I don't jump out of my skin or anything if a door slams. Speaking of doors, the front door at the parsonage is so drafty. It's this heavy oak thing, probably original to the house, which was built in like, 1920 or something. It has this beautiful stained glass window in the center, though. It's a picture of a dove with an olive branch. The colors are amazing when the sun hits it just right in the late afternoon. But the weather stripping is completely shot. I tried fixing it myself with this foam tape I bought at Home Depot, but it just peeled right off the next day. Home Depot is always so overwhelming anyway. The aisles are so long, and I can never find anyone in an orange apron to help me. I was in there looking for lightbulbs last week—just regular 60-watt bulbs for my reading lamp—and I ended up in the plumbing aisle looking at PVC pipes for twenty minutes. I don't even know anything about plumbing. My dad was always the handy one. He could fix anything with a roll of duct tape and a Swiss Army knife. He used to carry that knife everywhere, even to church... \n\nBut no, sudden noises don't really bother me. I haven't noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I guess I do. It's like I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. If I go to a restaurant, I have to be in a booth facing the entrance. I went to this diner last week, the one down on 4th Street that serves breakfast all day. They make these incredible blueberry pancakes, like, the blueberries are actually cooked into the batter, not just dumped on top. I hate when places just dump cold fruit on top of a hot pancake. It ruins the whole temperature dynamic, you know? Anyway, they tried to seat me in the middle of the room and I just... I couldn't do it. I had to ask for a corner booth. \n\nAnd at church events, it's really bad. I'm supposed to be fellowshipping, but I'm always watching people's hands, or scanning the corners of the room. If a car slows down outside the window, my eyes just dart right to it. I'm constantly mapping out where the nearest exit is instead of actually paying attention to the person talking to me. It's exhausting. Like, last Sunday, Mrs. Higgins was telling me about her grandson's piano recital. He's been playing for three years, apparently. I think he's learning some Chopin right now, which is pretty ambitious for a ten-year-old. Chopin has all those crazy trills and complex time signatures. I tried learning the \"Minute Waltz\" when I was a teenager, but my hands just aren't fast enough. You have to have really nimble fingers for that kind of stuff. My piano teacher, Mr. Davies, used to make me do these Hanon exercises for like thirty minutes before we even started a piece. He was so strict. He always smelled like peppermint and old paper... \n\nBut yeah, I'm always just... scanning. Watching. I can't just relax and be present.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before your cousin's death that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Man, I don't know. I used to love preaching, you know? Getting up there and sharing the Word, connecting with the congregation. And playing the keys during worship... I used to get so lost in the music. I actually have this really nice Roland keyboard at home. It's an RD-2000, fully weighted keys, so it feels exactly like a real grand piano. I saved up for like a year to buy it. I used to practice on it every night. The action on those keys is just incredible, and the built-in piano sounds are so rich. They sampled actual concert grands for it. I bought this heavy-duty stand for it, too, because it weighs a ton. It's one of those Z-stands, super stable. I got it online from Sweetwater. They always send a little bag of candy with your order, which is such a weird but cool marketing thing. I usually eat the Bit-O-Honey first. Bit-O-Honey is so underrated, honestly. People always go for the chocolate, but there's something about that chewy honey and almond flavor... \n\nBut yeah, the preaching, the keyboard... taking pictures, too. I used to do a lot of photography. And I'd play pickup basketball with the guys on the court behind the church. But now? Now I mostly just go through the motions. Or I avoid it altogether. None of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death itself, like you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. All the time. I keep... I keep telling myself that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, or if I had walked him out to his car, he'd still be here. Or if I had just kept a better watch over those boys... I was supposed to be in charge. I'm the youth pastor. I'm supposed to be the responsible one. Being a youth pastor is a lot harder than people think, you know? They think it's all just playing dodgeball and eating pizza. We used to order from this place called Tony's down on 5th Avenue. Best garlic knots in the city. They make them from scratch every morning, and they just drench them in this butter and garlic sauce. The owner, Tony—well, his name is actually Anthony, but everyone calls him Tony—he always gives us a discount because it's for the church. He's a really nice guy. He's got this huge mustache that looks like something out of an old western movie. My grandfather used to have a mustache like that. He was a mechanic. He worked on classic cars. He had this beautiful '67 Mustang in his garage that he spent years restoring. It was cherry red with white racing stripes. The engine sounded like a beast when you revved it. He taught me how to change the oil when I was like, eight years old. You have to make sure you put the drain plug back in tight, but not too tight, or you'll strip the threads. And getting the oil filter off can be a nightmare if it's stuck...\n\nBut yeah, I just... I should have done something differently. If I had just paid more attention, he wouldn't be gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always expecting something bad to happen, even when things seem to be going well?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I'm not really sure if I expect bad things all the time. I mean, it's hard to say what's normal anymore. Everything just feels kind of... off. Like when you're watching a movie and the audio is just a split second behind the video? That used to drive me crazy when I'd try to watch sermons online. We actually had to upgrade the whole A/V system at the church because the livestream kept lagging. We got this new digital mixer, a Behringer X32. It's got like 32 channels and motorized faders, which is super cool to watch when you switch scenes. The faders just zip up and down on their own. It took me like three weeks to figure out how to route the monitors properly. I had to watch all these YouTube tutorials from this guy who wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts. I don't know why, but sound engineers always seem to wear Hawaiian shirts or cargo shorts. It's like a uniform. My uncle used to wear cargo shorts all the time. He said he needed the pockets for all his tools, but he mostly just kept peppermints and golf tees in them. He loved golf. He'd go out every Sunday afternoon, even if it was raining. He said the rain made the greens softer, which helped his short game. I went with him once, but I'm terrible at golf. I always slice the ball into the woods...\n\nBut anyway, expecting bad things? I don't know. I just... I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent threat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. It's just... I have to see who's coming in. And when I'm at church events now, I'm not even paying attention to the people in front of me. I'm just always watching hands, looking at corners, noticing if a car slows down outside. I'm constantly scoping out where the nearest exit is. We actually have four exits in the main sanctuary, which is good for fire code. The fire marshal came by last month to do an inspection. He was this really gruff guy named Steve. He made us change out all the batteries in the emergency exit signs because they were like, a week past their expiration date. We had to buy these special 6-volt batteries that you can't just get at a regular grocery store. I had to drive all the way out to the industrial park to this specialty battery warehouse. It's this massive building, just aisles and aisles of batteries. They even had those huge ones for forklifts. Forklifts are so dangerous, honestly. My buddy Dave used to drive a forklift at a warehouse, and he said guys would always try to do tricks on them, like popping wheelies. Which is insane because those things weigh thousands of pounds. If one tips over on you, you're done. He said they had to watch these super cheesy safety videos from the 90s where the actors would dramatically overreact to getting their toes run over... \n\nBut yeah, I'm just always watching. Always looking for the exits, watching people's hands. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily irritated or angered by small things that wouldn't have bothered you before your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have been. It's bad. I've snapped in ministry meetings over things that shouldn't even matter. Just last week, I raised my voice at the deacons and a couple of the youth leaders over the scheduling for the fall retreat. I was talking to them like they were the enemy, you know? Just completely went off on them. And afterward, I just hate how fast I went there. I never used to be like that. The fall retreat is usually my favorite thing to plan. We go up to this camp in the mountains, Camp Cedar Ridge. It's beautiful up there in October when the leaves are changing. The camp director, his name is Pastor Mike, he's been running the place for like thirty years. He has this massive golden retriever named Samson who just wanders around the camp all day begging for hot dogs. The kids love him. We always do a big bonfire on the last night, and we make s'mores. But you have to be careful with the marshmallows because if you get them too close to the coals, they just turn into a fireball. My little sister used to do that on purpose when we were kids. She'd catch her marshmallow on fire and then wave it around like a torch until my dad yelled at her. He was always so strict about fire safety. He was a volunteer firefighter for a while in our hometown. They had this old siren on top of the firehouse that would go off whenever there was a call, and it was so loud you could hear it from miles away. It sounded like an air raid siren from World War II. I used to watch all these documentaries on the History Channel about World War II with my grandpa...\n\nBut yeah, the anger... I just snap at people now. I don't mean to, but it just happens so fast.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They sampled actual concert grands for it. I bought this heavy-duty stand for it, too, because it weighs a ton. It's one of those Z-stands, super stable. I got it online from Sweetwater. They always send a little bag of candy with your order, which is such a weird but cool marketing thing. I usually eat the Bit-O-Honey first. Bit-O-Honey is so underrated, honestly. People always go for the chocolate, but there's something about that chewy honey and almond flavor... \n\nBut yeah, the preaching, the keyboard... taking pictures, too. I used to do a lot of photography. And I'd play pickup basketball with the guys on the court behind the church. But now? Now I mostly just go through the motions. Or I avoid it altogether. None of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just... empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death itself, like you could have done something to prevent it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. All the time. I keep... I keep telling myself that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, or if I had walked him out to his car, he'd still be here. Or if I had just kept a better watch over those boys... I was supposed to be in charge. I'm the youth pastor. I'm supposed to be the responsible one. Being a youth pastor is a lot harder than people think, you know? They think it's all just playing dodgeball and eating pizza. We used to order from this place called Tony's down on 5th Avenue. Best garlic knots in the city. They make them from scratch every morning, and they just drench them in this butter and garlic sauce. The owner, Tony—well, his name is actually Anthony, but everyone calls him Tony—he always gives us a discount because it's for the church. He's a really nice guy. He's got this huge mustache that looks like something out of an old western movie. My grandfather used to have a mustache like that. He was a mechanic. He worked on classic cars. He had this beautiful '67 Mustang in his garage that he spent years restoring. It was cherry red with white racing stripes. The engine sounded like a beast when you revved it. He taught me how to change the oil when I was like, eight years old. You have to make sure you put the drain plug back in tight, but not too tight, or you'll strip the threads. And getting the oil filter off can be a nightmare if it's stuck...\n\nBut yeah, I just... I should have done something differently. If I had just paid more attention, he wouldn't be gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always expecting something bad to happen, even when things seem to be going well?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I'm not really sure if I expect bad things all the time. I mean, it's hard to say what's normal anymore. Everything just feels kind of... off. Like when you're watching a movie and the audio is just a split second behind the video? That used to drive me crazy when I'd try to watch sermons online. We actually had to upgrade the whole A/V system at the church because the livestream kept lagging. We got this new digital mixer, a Behringer X32. It's got like 32 channels and motorized faders, which is super cool to watch when you switch scenes. The faders just zip up and down on their own. It took me like three weeks to figure out how to route the monitors properly. I had to watch all these YouTube tutorials from this guy who wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts. I don't know why, but sound engineers always seem to wear Hawaiian shirts or cargo shorts. It's like a uniform. My uncle used to wear cargo shorts all the time. He said he needed the pockets for all his tools, but he mostly just kept peppermints and golf tees in them. He loved golf. He'd go out every Sunday afternoon, even if it was raining. He said the rain made the greens softer, which helped his short game. I went with him once, but I'm terrible at golf. I always slice the ball into the woods...\n\nBut anyway, expecting bad things? I don't know. I just... I don't really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly aware of your surroundings, even when there's no apparent threat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I'm just always watching hands, looking at corners, noticing if a car slows down outside. I'm constantly scoping out where the nearest exit is. We actually have four exits in the main sanctuary, which is good for fire code. The fire marshal came by last month to do an inspection. He was this really gruff guy named Steve. He made us change out all the batteries in the emergency exit signs because they were like, a week past their expiration date. We had to buy these special 6-volt batteries that you can't just get at a regular grocery store. I had to drive all the way out to the industrial park to this specialty battery warehouse. It's this massive building, just aisles and aisles of batteries. They even had those huge ones for forklifts. Forklifts are so dangerous, honestly. My buddy Dave used to drive a forklift at a warehouse, and he said guys would always try to do tricks on them, like popping wheelies. Which is insane because those things weigh thousands of pounds. If one tips over on you, you're done. He said they had to watch these super cheesy safety videos from the 90s where the actors would dramatically overreact to getting their toes run over... \n\nBut yeah, I'm just always watching. Always looking for the exits, watching people's hands. It's exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily irritated or angered by small things that wouldn't have bothered you before your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have been. It's bad. I've snapped in ministry meetings over things that shouldn't even matter. Just last week, I raised my voice at the deacons and a couple of the youth leaders over the scheduling for the fall retreat. I was talking to them like they were the enemy, you know? Just completely went off on them. And afterward, I just hate how fast I went there. I never used to be like that. The fall retreat is usually my favorite thing to plan. We go up to this camp in the mountains, Camp Cedar Ridge. It's beautiful up there in October when the leaves are changing. The camp director, his name is Pastor Mike, he's been running the place for like thirty years. He has this massive golden retriever named Samson who just wanders around the camp all day begging for hot dogs. The kids love him. We always do a big bonfire on the last night, and we make s'mores. But you have to be careful with the marshmallows because if you get them too close to the coals, they just turn into a fireball. My little sister used to do that on purpose when we were kids. He was a volunteer firefighter for a while in our hometown. They had this old siren on top of the firehouse that would go off whenever there was a call, and it was so loud you could hear it from miles away. It sounded like an air raid siren from World War II. Yeah, I think I just mentioned that, actually. It's like I was saying, I really can't even sit with my back to a door anymore. It's impossible. And when I'm at church events, I'm not even paying attention to the people I'm supposed to be talking to. Which is why I know we have four exits in the sanctuary. And like I said, the fire marshal, Steve, he was super strict about those exit signs. He actually told us this crazy story about a church he inspected a few years ago where they had chained the back doors shut from the inside to keep people from sneaking in late during the service. Can you imagine? If there had been a fire, it would have been a complete disaster. It's actually illegal to do that, it's a huge fire code violation. He said he threatened to shut the whole place down right then and there. I mean, you have to take that stuff seriously. Fire spreads so fast, especially in older buildings. Our church was built in the 70s, so there's a lot of old wood and carpet everywhere. The carpet in the fellowship hall is this hideous orange and brown shag. We've been trying to raise the money to replace it for like three years, but the building fund is always getting drained by other stuff, like the HVAC system. The AC unit on the roof broke down in the middle of July last year, and it was like an oven in there. We had to rent these massive industrial fans just to get through the Sunday service. The fans were so loud you could barely hear the worship band...

But yeah, the checking things... I just can't stop doing it. I'm always looking for the exits and watching people.
